And, well, the second I even think there might be a bat—or, you know, just something flapping around back there—my heart just completely jumps. I start sweating, and I just feel like I need to get out right then and there. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly for a guy my age to get so worked up over something like that, I'm sorry. Is that... is that the sort of thing you handle here?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or could it be other animals or things that flap or move unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's really just bats. Or, you know, things that I *think* might be bats. \n\nI mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn't actually going to hurt me. I'm well aware of how ridiculous it sounds for me to say this. But if I see anything that even looks like one, or if I just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual danger, even though logically I know better. \n\nIt's just that specific thing. Is that... does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear occur only when you see or think you see a bat, or also when you're in a place where you think bats might be, even if you don't see one?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's a bit of both? If I know I have to go somewhere that might have them, like the loading area or the back stairwell, I start getting really anxious. \n\nI've, uh... I've actually been trying to avoid those spots if I can. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, I really do try to get all my work done. But I've been swapping my closing shifts with some of the other guys so I don't have to be back there at night. And I'll take the long way around the store to avoid the loading dock. \n\nWhen I absolutely have to go into the back stairwell, I just... I make myself do it as fast as humanly possible. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and just feel completely terrified the whole time. \n\nI know I shouldn't be avoiding parts of my job, and I'm trying to handle it, I really am. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting other parts of your life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try to keep it from getting in the way of things. Outside of work, I live in a pretty nice apartment complex, so it's not like there are bats flying around the parking lot or anything. I guess if I'm out at night near the park, I might keep an eye on the sky, just to be safe. But I wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. \n\nI really try to keep it under control, you know? I don't want to be a bother to my friends or family with this. I hope that's okay to say... I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture without making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try really hard not to let it affect my friends or, you know, my social life. \n\nIf someone invites me to a barbecue or something in the evening, I still go! I might just... maybe I'll try to sit closer to the porch light or under an umbrella if they have one, just to feel a little more secure. But I definitely don't stay home or cancel plans because of it. I'd hate to be that guy who ruins a good time over something so silly. \n\nSo, yeah, socially I think I'm doing okay. I really try to just push through it so nobody else has to worry about it. Is that... I mean, does that sound like a normal way to handle it? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fine if I shouldn't be, but I really don't think it's impacting my social life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, um... I wouldn't say I completely avoid buildings. I mean, I really try to do my job, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone.

Well, um... I really wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. I mean, I still go to work, I still see my friends, I still do all the normal things people do. I really try to just... keep a positive attitude about it, you know? 

I guess... I mean, obviously the work thing is a little stressful right now, with the shift lead position and all. But I'm sure I can smooth that over with my manager. I don't want to make it sound like this huge, dramatic problem that's destroying everything. It's just a little hiccup, right? 

I really try to focus on the good things and not let this one fear take over. I mean, everyone has something they're scared of, don't they? I'm still a happy person overall, I promise. I just... I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.

I start sweating, and I just feel like I need to get out right then and there. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly for a guy my age to get so worked up over something like that, I'm sorry. Is that... is that the sort of thing you handle here?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or could it be other animals or things that flap or move unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's really just bats. Or, you know, things that I *think* might be bats. \n\nI mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn't actually going to hurt me. I'm well aware of how ridiculous it sounds for me to say this. But if I see anything that even looks like one, or if I just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual danger, even though logically I know better. \n\nIt's just that specific thing. Is that... does that make sense? But I've been swapping my closing shifts with some of the other guys so I don't have to be back there at night. And I'll take the long way around the store to avoid the loading dock. \n\nWhen I absolutely have to go into the back stairwell, I just... I make myself do it as fast as humanly possible. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and just feel completely terrified the whole time. \n\nI know I shouldn't be avoiding parts of my job, and I'm trying to handle it, I really am. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting other parts of your life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try really hard not to let it affect my friends or, you know, my social life. \n\nIf someone invites me to a barbecue or something in the evening, I still go! I might just... maybe I'll try to sit closer to the porch light or under an umbrella if they have one, just to feel a little more secure. But I definitely don't stay home or cancel plans because of it. I'd hate to be that guy who ruins a good time over something so silly. \n\nSo, yeah, socially I think I'm doing okay. I really try to just push through it so nobody else has to worry about it. Is that... I mean, does that sound like a normal way to handle it? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fine if I shouldn't be, but I really don't think it's impacting my social life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine in any significant way because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well, um... my day-to-day routine is mostly the same, I promise. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like you're missing out on things you enjoy because of your fear of bats?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My manager has started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role because I keep trading closing duties and I'm not as dependable for the after-hours checks. It feels like this could cost me the permanent management position I was working toward.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I mean, as far as fun stuff goes, not really. I still have a great time with my friends! But, um... I guess there is one thing at work that I'm a little worried about missing out on. I didn't really want to bring it up because I don't want you to think I'm slacking off or anything. I really do work hard! 

But... well, my manager—who is great, by the way, really observant—he kind of noticed that I keep trading those closing duties. And he brought up that I'm maybe not as, um, dependable for the after-hours checks in the back. So he actually pulled me aside and started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. 

Which is totally fair of him to ask, right? He has to look out for the store, I completely get it. But... it just kind of feels like this whole silly issue might actually cost me the permanent management position I've been working so hard toward. I just really want to do a good job and prove to them that I'm reliable. Do you think there's a way I can show him I'm still right for the promotion, even with this one little hang-up? I really don't want to let them down.

I really try to just push through it so nobody else has to worry about it. Is that... I mean, does that sound like a normal way to handle it? But it feels like this might actually cost me the permanent management position I was working toward. \n\nI'm trying so hard to fix it, I really am... I hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit. Is it... is it normal for something like this to get in the way of work like that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling to a specific location because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be flexible and go with the flow, especially if I'm traveling with other people. I wouldn't want to ruin a vacation or a trip just because of my... my issue. \n\nI guess if someone suggested going cave exploring or something, I might suggest we do something else instead? But I wouldn't make a big deal out of it. I'd probably just say I'm claustrophobic or something, just so I wouldn't have to explain the whole bat thing and make everyone uncomfortable. But as far as just traveling to a city or a state or something... no, I haven't avoided anything like that. I really do try to keep it from controlling my life. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just try to be easygoing about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided going to a specific type of building or structure because you thought it might have bats in it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I completely avoid buildings. I mean, I really try to do my job, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone. \n\nBut... I guess there are certain parts of the building at work. I usually hold my phone out like a flashlight, and my heart's just pounding the whole time. I'm always terrified something's going to swoop down. But I do get it done! I just... I really hope that doesn't make me sound completely crazy? I'm just trying to manage it the best I can without causing a fuss. Is that... is that the sort of thing you handle here?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or could it be other animals or things that flap or move unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's really just bats. Or, you know, things that I *think* might be bats. \n\nI mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn't actually going to hurt me. I'm well aware of how ridiculous it sounds for me to say this. But if I see anything that even looks like one, or if I just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual danger, even though logically I know better. \n\nIt's just that specific thing. Is that... does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear occur only when you see or think you see a bat, or also when you're in a place where you think bats might be, even if you don't see one?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's a bit of both? If I know I have to go somewhere that might have them, like the loading area or the back stairwell, I start getting really anxious. \n\nI've, uh... I've actually been trying to avoid those spots if I can. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, I really do try to get all my work done. But I've been swapping my closing shifts with some of the other guys so I don't have to be back there at night. And I'll take the long way around the store to avoid the loading dock. \n\nWhen I absolutely have to go into the back stairwell, I just... I make myself do it as fast as humanly possible. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and just feel completely terrified the whole time. \n\nI know I shouldn't be avoiding parts of my job, and I'm trying to handle it, I really am. I guess if I'm out at night near the park, I might keep an eye on the sky, just to be safe. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and just feel completely terrified the whole time. \n\nI know I shouldn't be avoiding parts of my job, and I'm trying to handle it, I really am. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting other parts of your life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try to keep it from getting in the way of things. Outside of work, I live in a pretty nice apartment complex, so it's not like there are bats flying around the parking lot or anything. I guess if I'm out at night near the park, I might keep an eye on the sky, just to be safe. But I wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. \n\nI really try to keep it under control, you know? I don't want to be a bother to my friends or family with this. I hope that's okay to say... I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture without making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try really hard not to let it affect my friends or, you know, my social life. \n\nIf someone invites me to a barbecue or something in the evening, I still go! I might just... maybe I'll try to sit closer to the porch light or under an umbrella if they have one, just to feel a little more secure. But I definitely don't stay home or cancel plans because of it. I'd hate to be that guy who ruins a good time over something so silly. \n\nSo, yeah, socially I think I'm doing okay. I really try to just push through it so nobody else has to worry about it. Is that... I mean, does that sound like a normal way to handle it? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fine if I shouldn't be, but I really don't think it's impacting my social life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine in any significant way because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well, um... my day-to-day routine is mostly the same, I promise. I try really hard to just keep things normal. But... I guess there is one thing. I feel a little embarrassed even bringing it up, because I really don't want you to think I'm a slacker or anything... \n\nIt's just... my manager noticed I've been trading those closing shifts I told you about. He actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I'm avoiding the after-hours checks out back, he told me I'm not being as dependable lately. It really hurts to hear that because I'm a hard worker, you know? But it feels like this might actually cost me the permanent management position I was working toward. \n\nI'm trying so hard to fix it, I really am... I hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit. Is it... is it normal for something like this to get in the way of work like that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling to a specific location because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be flexible and go with the flow, especially if I'm traveling with other people. I wouldn't want to ruin a vacation or a trip just because of my... my issue. \n\nI guess if someone suggested going cave exploring or something, I might suggest we do something else instead? But I wouldn't make a big deal out of it. I'd probably just say I'm claustrophobic or something, just so I wouldn't have to explain the whole bat thing and make everyone uncomfortable. But as far as just traveling to a city or a state or something... no, I haven't avoided anything like that. I really do try to keep it from controlling my life. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just try to be easygoing about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided going to a specific type of building or structure because you thought it might have bats in it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I completely avoid buildings. I mean, I really try to do my job, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone. \n\nBut... I guess there are certain parts of the building at work. Like I mentioned before, I've been swapping out those closing shifts so I don't have to go out back in the dark. And... well, I also kind of take the long way around the loading area now. Just to be safe. \n\nAnd, um, there's this one stairwell I have to check sometimes. I don't avoid it! I still do it, I promise. But... when I do, I kind of just run through it really fast. I usually hold my phone out like a flashlight, and my heart's just pounding the whole time. I'm always terrified something's going to swoop down. But I do get it done! I just... I really hope that doesn't make me sound completely crazy? I'm just trying to manage it the best I can without causing a fuss. Is it okay that I do that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. I mean, I still go to work, I still see my friends, I still do all the normal things people do. I really try to just... keep a positive attitude about it, you know? \n\nI guess... I mean, obviously the work thing is a little stressful right now, with the shift lead position and all. But I'm sure I can smooth that over with my manager. I don't want to make it sound like this huge, dramatic problem that's destroying everything. It's just a little hiccup, right? \n\nI really try to focus on the good things and not let this one fear take over. I mean, everyone has something they're scared of, don't they? I'm still a happy person overall, I promise. I just... I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because the location might have bats?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no. No, I really don't think I've ever done that. I try really hard to be a good friend, you know? I wouldn't want to skip out on a birthday or a barbecue just because of... well, my little issue. \n\nIf someone invited me somewhere and it was outside at night, I'd still go! I promise. I might just, you know, volunteer to help out inside the kitchen, or stay closer to where the lights are and where everyone else is gathered. But I'd never just flat-out reject an invite. That would be rude, right? I'd hate for anyone to think I didn't care about them or that I was being difficult. \n\nI always make sure to show up and be supportive. I think it's important to not let things like this affect your friendships, don't you agree? I just really try to be a good guest and not make it about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a specific event or gathering because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. I really try my best to be there for people, you know? Like I was saying about the social stuff, I wouldn't want to let anyone down or make them think I'm avoiding them. \n\nIf there's an event, I'll go. I mean, even if it's, say, an outdoor evening thing near some trees or something where... where they might be. I'd still go! I'd just, um, maybe try to stay under a tent if they have one, or stick close to the main group. I just try to blend in and be normal about it. I really don't want to be the guy who ruins a good time or makes everything complicated. I'm pretty easygoing, honestly. Is that the right way to handle it, do you think? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats has caused you to avoid doing something you wanted to do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess maybe a little bit? But nothing major, I promise. Like, at work, I've just been... sort of swapping out my closing shifts with some of the other guys. It's actually a win-win because they like the extra hours, you know? And if I have to take out the trash, I just take the long way around the loading dock instead of the shortcut by the old awning. It's just a few extra steps, really good for getting my steps in anyway!\n\nAnd... well, when I have to check the back stairwell before locking up, I still do it. I don't avoid it completely! I just... I make myself do it really fast. I'll hold my phone out with the flashlight on, and my heart might be beating a little fast, and I feel pretty scared the whole time, but I get it done. I'm still doing my job, right? I just have my own little ways of managing it so I don't cause any problems for anyone else. Does that sound okay to you? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on things you enjoy because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well... But... it just kind of feels like this whole silly issue might actually cost me the permanent management position I've been working so hard toward. I just really want to do a good job and prove to them that I'm reliable. Do you think there's a way I can show him I'm still right for the promotion, even with this one little hang-up? I really don't want to let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats affects how you plan your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, my daily routine? No, I really don't think so. I just want to make sure I'm keeping it all in perspective.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yeah, I guess that kind of goes back to what I was mentioning a little bit ago. Um, outdoor events? No, no, I don't think I've ever avoided anything like that. 

I mean, I go to barbecues, or, you know, maybe a baseball game if a friend invites me. I don't really think about bats when I'm doing those kinds of things. I start sweating, and I just feel like I need to get out right then and there. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly for a guy my age to get so worked up over something like that, I'm sorry. Is that... is that the sort of thing you handle here?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or could it be other animals or things that flap or move unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's really just bats. Or, you know, things that I *think* might be bats. \n\nI mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn't actually going to hurt me. I'm well aware of how ridiculous it sounds for me to say this. But if I see anything that even looks like one, or if I just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual danger, even though logically I know better. \n\nIt's just that specific thing. Is that... does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear occur only when you see or think you see a bat, or also when you're in a place where you think bats might be, even if you don't see one?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's a bit of both? If I know I have to go somewhere that might have them, like the loading area or the back stairwell, I start getting really anxious. \n\nI've, uh... I've actually been trying to avoid those spots if I can. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, I really do try to get all my work done. But I've been swapping my closing shifts with some of the other guys so I don't have to be back there at night. And I'll take the long way around the store to avoid the loading dock. \n\nWhen I absolutely have to go into the back stairwell, I just... I make myself do it as fast as humanly possible. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and just feel completely terrified the whole time. \n\nI know I shouldn't be avoiding parts of my job, and I'm trying to handle it, I really am. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting other parts of your life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try to keep it from getting in the way of things. Outside of work, I live in a pretty nice apartment complex, so it's not like there are bats flying around the parking lot or anything. I guess if I'm out at night near the park, I might keep an eye on the sky, just to be safe. But I wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. \n\nI really try to keep it under control, you know? I don't want to be a bother to my friends or family with this. I hope that's okay to say... I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture without making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try really hard not to let it affect my friends or, you know, my social life. \n\nIf someone invites me to a barbecue or something in the evening, I still go! I might just... maybe I'll try to sit closer to the porch light or under an umbrella if they have one, just to feel a little more secure. But I definitely don't stay home or cancel plans because of it. I'd hate to be that guy who ruins a good time over something so silly. \n\nSo, yeah, socially I think I'm doing okay. I really try to just push through it so nobody else has to worry about it. Is that... I mean, does that sound like a normal way to handle it? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fine if I shouldn't be, but I really don't think it's impacting my social life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine in any significant way because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well, um... my day-to-day routine is mostly the same, I promise. I try really hard to just keep things normal. But... I guess there is one thing. I feel a little embarrassed even bringing it up, because I really don't want you to think I'm a slacker or anything... \n\nIt's just... my manager noticed I've been trading those closing shifts I told you about. He actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I'm avoiding the after-hours checks out back, he told me I'm not being as dependable lately. It really hurts to hear that because I'm a hard worker, you know? But it feels like this might actually cost me the permanent management position I was working toward. \n\nI'm trying so hard to fix it, I really am... I hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit. Is it... is it normal for something like this to get in the way of work like that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling to a specific location because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be flexible and go with the flow, especially if I'm traveling with other people. I wouldn't want to ruin a vacation or a trip just because of my... my issue. \n\nI guess if someone suggested going cave exploring or something, I might suggest we do something else instead? But I wouldn't make a big deal out of it. I'd probably just say I'm claustrophobic or something, just so I wouldn't have to explain the whole bat thing and make everyone uncomfortable. But as far as just traveling to a city or a state or something... no, I haven't avoided anything like that. I really do try to keep it from controlling my life. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just try to be easygoing about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided going to a specific type of building or structure because you thought it might have bats in it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I completely avoid buildings. I mean, I really try to do my job, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone. \n\nBut... I guess there are certain parts of the building at work. Like I mentioned before, I've been swapping out those closing shifts so I don't have to go out back in the dark. And... well, I also kind of take the long way around the loading area now. Just to be safe. \n\nAnd, um, there's this one stairwell I have to check sometimes. I don't avoid it! I still do it, I promise. But... when I do, I kind of just run through it really fast. I usually hold my phone out like a flashlight, and my heart's just pounding the whole time. I'm always terrified something's going to swoop down. But I do get it done! I just... I really hope that doesn't make me sound completely crazy? I'm just trying to manage it the best I can without causing a fuss. Is it okay that I do that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really wouldn't say it's ruining my life or anything like that. I mean, I still go to work, I still see my friends, I still do all the normal things people do. I really try to just... keep a positive attitude about it, you know? \n\nI guess... I mean, obviously the work thing is a little stressful right now, with the shift lead position and all. But I'm sure I can smooth that over with my manager. I don't want to make it sound like this huge, dramatic problem that's destroying everything. It's just a little hiccup, right? \n\nI really try to focus on the good things and not let this one fear take over. I mean, everyone has something they're scared of, don't they? I'm still a happy person overall, I promise. I just... I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because the location might have bats?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no. No, I really don't think I've ever done that. I try really hard to be a good friend, you know? I wouldn't want to skip out on a birthday or a barbecue just because of... well, my little issue. \n\nIf someone invited me somewhere and it was outside at night, I'd still go! I promise. I might just, you know, volunteer to help out inside the kitchen, or stay closer to where the lights are and where everyone else is gathered. But I'd never just flat-out reject an invite. That would be rude, right? I'd hate for anyone to think I didn't care about them or that I was being difficult. \n\nI always make sure to show up and be supportive. I think it's important to not let things like this affect your friendships, don't you agree? I just really try to be a good guest and not make it about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a specific event or gathering because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. I really try my best to be there for people, you know? Like I was saying about the social stuff, I wouldn't want to let anyone down or make them think I'm avoiding them. \n\nIf there's an event, I'll go. I mean, even if it's, say, an outdoor evening thing near some trees or something where... where they might be. I'd still go! I'd just, um, maybe try to stay under a tent if they have one, or stick close to the main group. I just try to blend in and be normal about it. I really don't want to be the guy who ruins a good time or makes everything complicated. I'm pretty easygoing, honestly. Is that the right way to handle it, do you think? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats has caused you to avoid doing something you wanted to do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess maybe a little bit? But nothing major, I promise. Like, at work, I've just been... sort of swapping out my closing shifts with some of the other guys. It's actually a win-win because they like the extra hours, you know? And if I have to take out the trash, I just take the long way around the loading dock instead of the shortcut by the old awning. It's just a few extra steps, really good for getting my steps in anyway!\n\nAnd... well, when I have to check the back stairwell before locking up, I still do it. I don't avoid it completely! I just... I make myself do it really fast. I'll hold my phone out with the flashlight on, and my heart might be beating a little fast, and I feel pretty scared the whole time, but I get it done. I'm still doing my job, right? I just have my own little ways of managing it so I don't cause any problems for anyone else. Does that sound okay to you? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on things you enjoy because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, as far as fun stuff goes, not really. I still have a great time with my friends! But, um... I guess there is one thing at work that I'm a little worried about missing out on. I didn't really want to bring it up because I don't want you to think I'm slacking off or anything. I really do work hard! \n\nBut... well, my manager—who is great, by the way, really observant—he kind of noticed that I keep trading those closing duties. And he brought up that I'm maybe not as, um, dependable for the after-hours checks in the back. So he actually pulled me aside and started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. \n\nWhich is totally fair of him to ask, right? He has to look out for the store, I completely get it. But... it just kind of feels like this whole silly issue might actually cost me the permanent management position I've been working so hard toward. I just really want to do a good job and prove to them that I'm reliable. Do you think there's a way I can show him I'm still right for the promotion, even with this one little hang-up? I really don't want to let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of bats affects how you plan your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, my daily routine? No, I really don't think so. I mean, besides what I just mentioned about the closing shifts at work, I just... I go about my day like anyone else. \n\nI get up, run my errands, do things around the house. I definitely don't let it take over my whole schedule or anything. I'd hate for it to get in the way of just living normally, you know? I really try to keep things as standard and productive as possible so I'm not inconveniencing anyone or being weird about things. So, no, I don't really plan my day-to-day life around it at all. \n\nThat's a good sign, right? Like, it means I'm still functioning pretty well overall? I just want to make sure I'm keeping it all in perspective.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yeah, I guess that kind of goes back to what I was mentioning a little bit ago. I really try my best not to just flat-out refuse to do things, because I don't want to be a burden on the team. \n\nBut... yeah, if I'm being completely honest, I have been trading away those closing shifts whenever I can. And with the trash, I'll purposefully take that long detour all the way around the loading area just to avoid the awning. I mean, I still make sure the trash gets taken out! I'm not avoiding the actual work, I promise. \n\nAnd, um, like I said about the back stairwell... I don't skip checking it, even though I really want to. I just sort of... force myself to do it. I'll grip my phone super tight so I have the flashlight on, and I just rush through it as fast as humanly possible because I'm just so terrified the whole time I'm in there. But the job still gets done! I really do try to pull my weight, even when I'm scared. That makes sense, right? I really hope it doesn't sound like I'm just making up excuses to get out of doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a specific store or building outside of work because you thought it might have bats in it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. I can't really think of any time I've avoided a store or a building outside of work just because of that. \n\nI guess... well, most places I go to are pretty normal, you know? Like the grocery store or the bank, they're all brightly lit and busy, so I don't really worry about it there. I try to just go about my normal errands without being difficult or, you know, making a fuss about where I can and can't go. I'd hate to be that guy who won't go into a perfectly fine building for no good reason. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty sure I don't do that. That's a normal way to handle it, right? I mean, it's good that I'm not letting it stop me from going places outside of work, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a specific type of outdoor event because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: Um, outdoor events? No, no, I don't think I've ever avoided anything like that. \n\nI mean, I go to barbecues, or, you know, maybe a baseball game if a friend invites me. I don't really think about bats when I'm doing those kinds of things. I guess most of the outdoor stuff I do is during the day anyway, or in places with a lot of people and lights, so it just doesn't cross my mind. \n\nI really try to just go along with what everyone else wants to do. I wouldn't want to ruin a fun outing or make my friends change their plans just because I'm feeling anxious about something silly. That would be pretty unfair to them, you know? \n\nSo, yeah, I definitely don't avoid outdoor events. That shows I'm keeping a pretty good balance, right? Like, I'm not letting this thing control my social life?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
